My heart jumps right up in my chest, my hands start to shake, and I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away. It's like a panic takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other insects like bees, or is it mainly just wasps that cause this reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the wasps. I mean, bees don't bother me much at all. They just kind of do their own thing with the flowers. It's just... yeah, mainly just wasps that make me feel that way. I don't really have a problem with other bugs.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other situations or places because you think you might encounter wasps?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a bit earlier, I had to step back from the church gardening group. But it's more than just that. I actually stopped hosting my own garden days at the house, too. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with people, so it's been hard giving that up. \n\nAnd around the house... well, I just avoid the yard altogether now. My husband has pretty much had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself, just because I can't risk being out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings because you think there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, if it's an outdoor barbecue in the middle of summer or something, I might think twice or ask if we can eat inside. But I don't avoid social gatherings altogether. If it's indoors or somewhere I know wasps won't be a problem, I'm perfectly fine going. It's really just about avoiding the places where they actually are, not avoiding people.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress as a result of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what you mean by significant distress. I mean... I'm not falling apart or crying about it all the time or anything like that. It's just really frustrating. \n\nI hate that I had to give up my gardening and that I can't even enjoy my own backyard anymore without feeling on edge. So it definitely bothers me a lot, and it makes me sad to miss out on those things, but I don't know if I'd use the word distress. It's just... a really big annoyance that I have to work my life around right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you still manage to do despite your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, of course. I mean, I still do all my normal stuff inside the house. I cook, I clean, I run errands, go to the grocery store, that kind of thing. As long as I'm indoors or just going straight from the house to the car, I'm fine. It's really just the yard work and the outdoor stuff that I've had to stop doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to change your daily routine significantly because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I used to be really involved with the church gardening group, but I've had to step back from that completely. I also stopped hosting garden days at my house, which... well, that used to be a big part of how I stayed connected with people. And just around my own home, my husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself. So it's definitely changed how I go about my days.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided traveling to certain locations because you thought you might encounter wasps there?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Honestly, that's really why I'm here. I've gotten to where I plan my whole summer around avoiding wasps. If I think they're going to be somewhere, I just don't go. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier, I gave up my garden jobs at the church, so I completely avoid going over there for that now. And it's even certain spots around my own house. If I hear buzzing, I just won't go near it. I'll actually make my husband unload things by the back porch because I refuse to be out there. So, yes, I definitely steer clear of anywhere I think they might be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers, it's just instant. It's like a panic takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other insects like bees, or is it mainly just wasps that cause this reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the wasps. I’ve basically gotten to the point where I plan my whole summer around avoiding wasps. \n\nIt's really starting to interfere with things. I even gave up my garden jobs at the church because of it. And at home... well, if I hear even a little buzzing, I make my husband unload things by the back porch. I just can't be out there. So, I figured I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When you see a wasp or hear its buzzing, do you feel an intense fear or panic?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers, it's just instant. My heart jumps right up in my chest, my hands start to shake, and I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away. It's like a panic takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other insects like bees, or is it mainly just wasps that cause this reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the wasps. I mean, bees don't bother me much at all. They just kind of do their own thing with the flowers. It's just... yeah, mainly just wasps that make me feel that way. I don't really have a problem with other bugs.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other situations or places because you think you might encounter wasps?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a bit earlier, I had to step back from the church gardening group. But it's more than just that. I actually stopped hosting my own garden days at the house, too. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with people, so it's been hard giving that up. \n\nAnd around the house... well, I just avoid the yard altogether now. My husband has pretty much had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself, just because I can't risk being out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings because you think there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, if it's an outdoor barbecue in the middle of summer or something, I might think twice or ask if we can eat inside. But I don't avoid social gatherings altogether. If it's indoors or somewhere I know wasps won't be a problem, I'm perfectly fine going. It's really just about avoiding the places where they actually are, not avoiding people.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress as a result of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what you mean by significant distress. I mean... I'm not falling apart or crying about it all the time or anything like that. It's just really frustrating. \n\nI hate that I had to give up my gardening and that I can't even enjoy my own backyard anymore without feeling on edge. So it definitely bothers me a lot, and it makes me sad to miss out on those things, but I don't know if I'd use the word distress. It's just... a really big annoyance that I have to work my life around right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you still manage to do despite your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, of course. I mean, I still do all my normal stuff inside the house. I cook, I clean, I run errands, go to the grocery store, that kind of thing. As long as I'm indoors or just going straight from the house to the car, I'm fine. I used to be really involved with the church gardening group, but I've had to step back from that completely. I also stopped hosting garden days at my house, which... well, that used to be a big part of how I stayed connected with people. And just around my own home, my husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself. So it's definitely changed how I go about my days.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided traveling to certain locations because you thought you might encounter wasps there?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Honestly, that's really why I'm here. I've gotten to where I plan my whole summer around avoiding wasps. If I think they're going to be somewhere, I just don't go. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier, I gave up my garden jobs at the church, so I completely avoid going over there for that now. And it's even certain spots around my own house. If I hear buzzing, I just won't go near it. I'll actually make my husband unload things by the back porch because I refuse to be out there. So, yes, I definitely steer clear of anywhere I think they might be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided going on vacation or traveling to a specific location during a particular time of year because you thought there might be wasps there?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's mostly just local things, like I said, the church and my own yard.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps limited to specific situations or contexts, such as when you are outdoors or near flowers?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is mostly when I'm outdoors in those kinds of spots. Like you said, near the flowers, or out by the porch. It's just... the second I hear that sharp buzzing sound, or if I see one hovering near the plants, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. So, yes, it's very tied to being outside in those specific areas where they usually are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of a wasp if it was dead or in a picture?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, if it's dead, it can't fly at me or sting me, so that wouldn't really bother me. And a picture is just a picture. My heart jumps right up in my chest, my hands start to shake, and I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away. It's like a panic takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other insects like bees, or is it mainly just wasps that cause this reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the wasps. I mean, bees don't bother me much at all. They just kind of do their own thing with the flowers. It's just... yeah, mainly just wasps that make me feel that way. I don't really have a problem with other bugs.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other situations or places because you think you might encounter wasps?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a bit earlier, I had to step back from the church gardening group. But it's more than just that. I actually stopped hosting my own garden days at the house, too. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with people, so it's been hard giving that up. \n\nAnd around the house... well, I just avoid the yard altogether now. My husband has pretty much had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself, just because I can't risk being out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings because you think there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, if it's an outdoor barbecue in the middle of summer or something, I might think twice or ask if we can eat inside. But I don't avoid social gatherings altogether. If it's indoors or somewhere I know wasps won't be a problem, I'm perfectly fine going. It's really just about avoiding the places where they actually are, not avoiding people.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress as a result of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what you mean by significant distress. I mean... I'm not falling apart or crying about it all the time or anything like that. It's just really frustrating. \n\nI hate that I had to give up my gardening and that I can't even enjoy my own backyard anymore without feeling on edge. So it definitely bothers me a lot, and it makes me sad to miss out on those things, but I don't know if I'd use the word distress. It's just... a really big annoyance that I have to work my life around right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you still manage to do despite your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, of course. I mean, I still do all my normal stuff inside the house. I cook, I clean, I run errands, go to the grocery store, that kind of thing. As long as I'm indoors or just going straight from the house to the car, I'm fine. It's really just the yard work and the outdoor stuff that I've had to stop doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to change your daily routine significantly because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I used to be really involved with the church gardening group, but I've had to step back from that completely. I also stopped hosting garden days at my house, which... well, that used to be a big part of how I stayed connected with people. And just around my own home, my husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself. So it's definitely changed how I go about my days.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided traveling to certain locations because you thought you might encounter wasps there?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Honestly, that's really why I'm here. I've gotten to where I plan my whole summer around avoiding wasps. If I think they're going to be somewhere, I just don't go. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier, I gave up my garden jobs at the church, so I completely avoid going over there for that now. And it's even certain spots around my own house. If I hear buzzing, I just won't go near it. I'll actually make my husband unload things by the back porch because I refuse to be out there. So, yes, I definitely steer clear of anywhere I think they might be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided going on vacation or traveling to a specific location during a particular time of year because you thought there might be wasps there?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't really travel much anyway, to be honest. We mostly just stay around town or visit family indoors, so I haven't really had to cancel a vacation over it or anything like that. It's mostly just local things, like I said, the church and my own yard.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps limited to specific situations or contexts, such as when you are outdoors or near flowers?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is mostly when I'm outdoors in those kinds of spots. Like you said, near the flowers, or out by the porch. It's just... the second I hear that sharp buzzing sound, or if I see one hovering near the plants, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. So, yes, it's very tied to being outside in those specific areas where they usually are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of a wasp if it was dead or in a picture?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, if it's dead, it can't fly at me or sting me, so that wouldn't really bother me. And a picture is just a picture. It's really just the live ones—the flying and the buzzing—that get to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you be afraid of a wasp if you were indoors and someone just talked about wasps or showed you a video of them?\n\nPatient: No, just talking about them doesn't bother me. And a video... well, it might make me a little uncomfortable if it's a really close-up shot or something, but I wouldn't say I'd be afraid. As long as I know they aren't actually in the room with me, I'm fine. It's really just the real, live ones that I can't handle.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of wasps because you think they might sting you?\n\nPatient: Well, getting stung is part of it, sure. But... it's really more than just that. I mean, I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would just swat it away without a second thought. But to me, it just feels like... like something terrible is about to happen if one gets anywhere near me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's mostly just local things, like I said, the church and my own yard.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps limited to specific situations or contexts, such as when you are outdoors or near flowers?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is mostly when I'm outdoors in those kinds of spots. Like you said, near the flowers, or out by the porch. It's just... the second I hear that sharp buzzing sound, or if I see one hovering near the plants, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. So, yes, it's very tied to being outside in those specific areas where they usually are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of a wasp if it was dead or in a picture?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, if it's dead, it can't fly at me or sting me, so that wouldn't really bother me. And a picture is just a picture. It's really just the live ones—the flying and the buzzing—that get to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you be afraid of a wasp if you were indoors and someone just talked about wasps or showed you a video of them?\n\nPatient: No, just talking about them doesn't bother me. And a video... well, it might make me a little uncomfortable if it's a really close-up shot or something, but I wouldn't say I'd be afraid. As long as I know they aren't actually in the room with me, I'm fine. It's really just the real, live ones that I can't handle.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of wasps because you think they might sting you?\n\nPatient: Well, getting stung is part of it, sure. But... it's really more than just that. I mean, I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would just swat it away without a second thought. But to me, it just feels like... like something terrible is about to happen if one gets anywhere near me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the possibility of being stung or about seeing or knowing that wasps are present?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, getting stung is definitely part of it, but... I don't know if I can separate the two things. I mean, just seeing one or knowing it's around is enough to set me off. It's the flying, the buzzing, the being in the same space as me. So, I guess it's both? I just know I can't be around them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because things have just gotten a bit out of hand. I’ve basically gotten to the point where I plan my whole summer around avoiding wasps. \n\nIt's really starting to interfere with things. I even gave up my garden jobs at the church because of it. And at home... well, if I hear even a little buzzing, I make my husband unload things by the back porch. I just can't be out there. So, I figured I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When you see a wasp or hear its buzzing, do you feel an intense fear or panic?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers, it's just instant. My heart jumps right up in my chest, my hands start to shake, and I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away. It's like a panic takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other insects like bees, or is it mainly just wasps that cause this reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the wasps. I mean, bees don't bother me much at all. They just kind of do their own thing with the flowers. It's just... yeah, mainly just wasps that make me feel that way. I don't really have a problem with other bugs.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other situations or places because you think you might encounter wasps?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a bit earlier, I had to step back from the church gardening group. But it's more than just that. I actually stopped hosting my own garden days at the house, too. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with people, so it's been hard giving that up. \n\nAnd around the house... well, I just avoid the yard altogether now. My husband has pretty much had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself, just because I can't risk being out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings because you think there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, if it's an outdoor barbecue in the middle of summer or something, I might think twice or ask if we can eat inside. But I don't avoid social gatherings altogether. If it's indoors or somewhere I know wasps won't be a problem, I'm perfectly fine going. It's really just about avoiding the places where they actually are, not avoiding people.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress as a result of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what you mean by significant distress. I mean... I'm not falling apart or crying about it all the time or anything like that. It's just really frustrating. \n\nI hate that I had to give up my gardening and that I can't even enjoy my own backyard anymore without feeling on edge. So it definitely bothers me a lot, and it makes me sad to miss out on those things, but I don't know if I'd use the word distress. It's just... a really big annoyance that I have to work my life around right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you still manage to do despite your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, of course. I mean, I still do all my normal stuff inside the house. I cook, I clean, I run errands, go to the grocery store, that kind of thing. And just around my own home, my husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because things have just gotten a bit out of hand. I’ve basically gotten to the point where I plan my whole summer around avoiding wasps. \n\nIt's really starting to interfere with things. I even gave up my garden jobs at the church because of it. And at home... well, if I hear even a little buzzing, I make my husband unload things by the back porch. I just can't be out there. So, I figured I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When you see a wasp or hear its buzzing, do you feel an intense fear or panic?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers, it's just instant. My heart jumps right up in my chest, my hands start to shake, and I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away. It's like a panic takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other insects like bees, or is it mainly just wasps that cause this reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the wasps. I mean, bees don't bother me much at all. They just kind of do their own thing with the flowers. It's just... yeah, mainly just wasps that make me feel that way. I don't really have a problem with other bugs.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other situations or places because you think you might encounter wasps?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a bit earlier, I had to step back from the church gardening group. But it's more than just that. I actually stopped hosting my own garden days at the house, too. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with people, so it's been hard giving that up. \n\nAnd around the house... well, I just avoid the yard altogether now. My husband has pretty much had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself, just because I can't risk being out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings because you think there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, if it's an outdoor barbecue in the middle of summer or something, I might think twice or ask if we can eat inside. But I don't avoid social gatherings altogether. If it's indoors or somewhere I know wasps won't be a problem, I'm perfectly fine going. It's really just about avoiding the places where they actually are, not avoiding people.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress as a result of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what you mean by significant distress. I mean... I'm not falling apart or crying about it all the time or anything like that. It's just really frustrating. \n\nI hate that I had to give up my gardening and that I can't even enjoy my own backyard anymore without feeling on edge. So it definitely bothers me a lot, and it makes me sad to miss out on those things, but I don't know if I'd use the word distress. It's just... a really big annoyance that I have to work my life around right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you still manage to do despite your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, of course. I mean, I still do all my normal stuff inside the house. I cook, I clean, I run errands, go to the grocery store, that kind of thing. As long as I'm indoors or just going straight from the house to the car, I'm fine. It's really just the yard work and the outdoor stuff that I've had to stop doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to change your daily routine significantly because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I used to be really involved with the church gardening group, but I've had to step back from that completely. I also stopped hosting garden days at my house, which... well, that used to be a big part of how I stayed connected with people. And just around my own home, my husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself. So it's definitely changed how I go about my days.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided traveling to certain locations because you thought you might encounter wasps there?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Honestly, that's really why I'm here. I've gotten to where I plan my whole summer around avoiding wasps. If I think they're going to be somewhere, I just don't go. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier, I gave up my garden jobs at the church, so I completely avoid going over there for that now. And it's even certain spots around my own house. If I hear buzzing, I just won't go near it. I'll actually make my husband unload things by the back porch because I refuse to be out there. So, yes, I definitely steer clear of anywhere I think they might be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided going on vacation or traveling to a specific location during a particular time of year because you thought there might be wasps there?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't really travel much anyway, to be honest. We mostly just stay around town or visit family indoors, so I haven't really had to cancel a vacation over it or anything like that. It's mostly just local things, like I said, the church and my own yard.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps limited to specific situations or contexts, such as when you are outdoors or near flowers?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is mostly when I'm outdoors in those kinds of spots. Like you said, near the flowers, or out by the porch. It's just... the second I hear that sharp buzzing sound, or if I see one hovering near the plants, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. So, yes, it's very tied to being outside in those specific areas where they usually are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of a wasp if it was dead or in a picture?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, if it's dead, it can't fly at me or sting me, so that wouldn't really bother me. And a picture is just a picture. It's really just the live ones—the flying and the buzzing—that get to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you be afraid of a wasp if you were indoors and someone just talked about wasps or showed you a video of them?\n\nPatient: No, just talking about them doesn't bother me. And a video... well, it might make me a little uncomfortable if it's a really close-up shot or something, but I wouldn't say I'd be afraid. As long as I know they aren't actually in the room with me, I'm fine. It's really just the real, live ones that I can't handle.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of wasps because you think they might sting you?\n\nPatient: Well, getting stung is part of it, sure. I don't really have a different answer for you, honestly. I'm not sure how else to explain it—it's just the whole package that terrifies me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps only because you believe they might sting you?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles here. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’m here because things have just gotten a bit out of hand. I’ve basically gotten to the point where I plan my whole summer around avoiding wasps. \n\nIt's really starting to interfere with things. I even gave up my garden jobs at the church because of it. And at home... well, if I hear even a little buzzing, I make my husband unload things by the back porch. I just can't be out there. So, I figured I needed to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: When you see a wasp or hear its buzzing, do you feel an intense fear or panic?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering near the flowers, it's just instant. My heart jumps right up in my chest, my hands start to shake, and I just feel this overwhelming need to get inside right away. It's like a panic takes over.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other insects like bees, or is it mainly just wasps that cause this reaction?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just the wasps. I mean, bees don't bother me much at all. They just kind of do their own thing with the flowers. It's just... yeah, mainly just wasps that make me feel that way. I don't really have a problem with other bugs.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding other situations or places because you think you might encounter wasps?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I mentioned a bit earlier, I had to step back from the church gardening group. But it's more than just that. I actually stopped hosting my own garden days at the house, too. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with people, so it's been hard giving that up. \n\nAnd around the house... well, I just avoid the yard altogether now. My husband has pretty much had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself, just because I can't risk being out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings because you think there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, if it's an outdoor barbecue in the middle of summer or something, I might think twice or ask if we can eat inside. But I don't avoid social gatherings altogether. If it's indoors or somewhere I know wasps won't be a problem, I'm perfectly fine going. It's really just about avoiding the places where they actually are, not avoiding people.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress as a result of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what you mean by significant distress. I mean... I'm not falling apart or crying about it all the time or anything like that. It's just really frustrating. \n\nI hate that I had to give up my gardening and that I can't even enjoy my own backyard anymore without feeling on edge. So it definitely bothers me a lot, and it makes me sad to miss out on those things, but I don't know if I'd use the word distress. It's just... a really big annoyance that I have to work my life around right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you still manage to do despite your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, of course. I mean, I still do all my normal stuff inside the house. I cook, I clean, I run errands, go to the grocery store, that kind of thing. As long as I'm indoors or just going straight from the house to the car, I'm fine. It's really just the yard work and the outdoor stuff that I've had to stop doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to change your daily routine significantly because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I used to be really involved with the church gardening group, but I've had to step back from that completely. I also stopped hosting garden days at my house, which... well, that used to be a big part of how I stayed connected with people. And just around my own home, my husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself. So it's definitely changed how I go about my days.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided traveling to certain locations because you thought you might encounter wasps there?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Honestly, that's really why I'm here. I've gotten to where I plan my whole summer around avoiding wasps. If I think they're going to be somewhere, I just don't go. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier, I gave up my garden jobs at the church, so I completely avoid going over there for that now. And it's even certain spots around my own house. If I hear buzzing, I just won't go near it. I'll actually make my husband unload things by the back porch because I refuse to be out there. So, yes, I definitely steer clear of anywhere I think they might be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided going on vacation or traveling to a specific location during a particular time of year because you thought there might be wasps there?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I don't really travel much anyway, to be honest. We mostly just stay around town or visit family indoors, so I haven't really had to cancel a vacation over it or anything like that. It's mostly just local things, like I said, the church and my own yard.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps limited to specific situations or contexts, such as when you are outdoors or near flowers?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is mostly when I'm outdoors in those kinds of spots. Like you said, near the flowers, or out by the porch. It's just... the second I hear that sharp buzzing sound, or if I see one hovering near the plants, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. So, yes, it's very tied to being outside in those specific areas where they usually are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of a wasp if it was dead or in a picture?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, if it's dead, it can't fly at me or sting me, so that wouldn't really bother me. And a picture is just a picture. It's really just the live ones—the flying and the buzzing—that get to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you be afraid of a wasp if you were indoors and someone just talked about wasps or showed you a video of them?\n\nPatient: No, just talking about them doesn't bother me. And a video... well, it might make me a little uncomfortable if it's a really close-up shot or something, but I wouldn't say I'd be afraid. As long as I know they aren't actually in the room with me, I'm fine. It's really just the real, live ones that I can't handle.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of wasps because you think they might sting you?\n\nPatient: Well, getting stung is part of it, sure. But... it's really more than just that. I mean, I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would just swat it away without a second thought. But to me, it just feels like... like something terrible is about to happen if one gets anywhere near me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps more about the possibility of being stung or about seeing or knowing that wasps are present?\n\nPatient: Well, like I said, getting stung is definitely part of it, but... I don't know if I can separate the two things. I mean, just seeing one or knowing it's around is enough to set me off. It's the flying, the buzzing, the being in the same space as me. So, I guess it's both? I just know I can't be around them.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps primarily about the act of being stung or is it more about the presence of wasps themselves?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, I really can't separate the two. I guess... I mean, just them being there is enough to make me panic. So it is their presence, definitely, but I'm also scared of what they might do. I don't really have a different answer for you, honestly. I'm not sure how else to explain it—it's just the whole package that terrifies me.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps only because you believe they might sting you?\n\nPatient: I feel like we're going in circles here. Like I said, the stinging is part of it, but it's not the only thing. Just... just having them around is enough to make me feel like I need to run away. I don't know how to make it any clearer than that.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything about wasps that scares you other than the possibility of being stung?\n\nPatient: I really don't know what else to tell you. I don't have some other specific reason they scare me, if that's what you're asking. It's just the buzzing, the flying, the stinging... just them being near me. There isn't anything else besides what I've already said.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That used to be a really big part of how I stayed connected with people, so it's been hard giving that up. \n\nAnd around the house... well, I just avoid the yard altogether now. My husband has pretty much had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself, just because I can't risk being out there.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid social gatherings because you think there might be wasps present?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If it's indoors or somewhere I know wasps won't be a problem, I'm perfectly fine going. It's really just about avoiding the places where they actually are, not avoiding people.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant distress as a result of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what you mean by significant distress. It's just really frustrating. \n\nI hate that I had to give up my gardening and that I can't even enjoy my own backyard anymore without feeling on edge. It's just... a really big annoyance that I have to work my life around right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities that you still manage to do despite your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, of course. I mean, I still do all my normal stuff inside the house. I cook, I clean, I run errands, go to the grocery store, that kind of thing. As long as I'm indoors or just going straight from the house to the car, I'm fine. It's really just the yard work and the outdoor stuff that I've had to stop doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to change your daily routine significantly because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I used to be really involved with the church gardening group, but I've had to step back from that completely. And just around my own home, my husband has had to take over all the porch and garden tasks that I used to handle myself. So it's definitely changed how I go about my days.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided traveling to certain locations because you thought you might encounter wasps there?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Honestly, that's really why I'm here. I've gotten to where I plan my whole summer around avoiding wasps. If I think they're going to be somewhere, I just don't go. \n\nLike I mentioned earlier, I gave up my garden jobs at the church, so I completely avoid going over there for that now. Like you said, near the flowers, or out by the porch. It's just... the second I hear that sharp buzzing sound, or if I see one hovering near the plants, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. So, yes, it's very tied to being outside in those specific areas where they usually are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be afraid of a wasp if it was dead or in a picture?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, if it's dead, it can't fly at me or sting me, so that wouldn't really bother me. And a picture is just a picture. It's really just the live ones—the flying and the buzzing—that get to me.\n\nDoctor: Would you be afraid of a wasp if you were indoors and someone just talked about wasps or showed you a video of them?\n\nPatient: No, just talking about them doesn't bother me.     "duration_verified": "symptoms have been present for an extended period, significantly impacting daily life and activities",
    "functional_impairment": true,
    "traumatic_stressor": null,
    "psychosocial_stressor": null,
    "additional_requirements": [
      "fear is persistent and excessive",
      "fear is not better explained by another mental disorder"
    ]
  }
}
